INTRODUCTORY NOTE 


IN presenting the second and concluding instalment of this 
strange old manual in its first European edition, it is with 
gratification and with gratitude that we here record, and 
both in this Note and in an Appendix embody, the response 


made to the appeal thrown out in our Introductory Note to . 


the former volume. We there, in view of the repelling 
method of the work, cited the words of the Commentary 
attributed to Buddhaghosa, in which the baffled student is 
advised to wait on teachers (p. xv). And we suggested 
that for ourselves, at this time of day, it was conceivable 
that help might come from the Vihiras of Burma. 

I wrote to Mr. Shwe Zan Aung, whose translation of the 
Abhidhammattha-Sangaha has found a steadily growing 
number of readers, but whose opportunities of increasing 
the indebtedness of Pali students to him are now 80 
curtailed by his professional duties. I stated what seemed 
to me some of the perplexities concerning the object and 
method of the book most likely to be felt by a European 
conversant with the main objects and methods of Pali 
Buddhist doctrine, and capable of appreciating the same. 
I gave expression to these perplexities in some half-dozen 
questions, very informally and roughly drawn up, I fear, 
concerning the Yamaka, more or less after the fashion of 
the Angelic Doctor's questions when starting on his Summa 
Theologie : Utrum sit scientia? utrum sit speculativa. vel 
practica? utrum sit sapientia! . . . argumentativa? quid sit 
subjectum eiua? . . . And I inquired if he (Mr. Aung) 
could favour me with any traditional or original views on 
the object, scope, and method of the Yamaka held and 
taught at the present day in his country. 

In the course of last year I received in reply, not only & 

vil 


viii Introductory Note 


few notes quoting eub-commentaries on the Yamaka, from 
the pen of Mr. Aung himself, but also detailed answers 
from two distinguished Burmese teachers : Ingan Sadaw 
(Dr. Ingan) of Visuddharama, the head of the Shwegyin 
School (or sect), and Ledi Sadaw of Mandalay, the well- 
known author, в list of whose twenty and more works is 
given in Mrs. Bode's ‘ Pali Literature in Burma,’ p. 97 /. 

Mr. Aung's notes are, of course, written in English, but 
in the replies of the two Theras my questions have been 
converted and modified into an approximate Pali, the 
answers being also in that language. The former scholar 
and Mr. Aung have replied with the degree of conciseness 
which was the utmost I had expected to be favoured with, 
but Ledi Sadaw has been so good as to write more than 
mere replies, taking some of the points raised as his 
themes. His disquisitions, couched in nervous, lucid 
Pali, are not citations from ‘Sika literature, but quote only 
from the Pitakas, and embody much that is instructive 
and illuminating on several points of doctrine—eg., on 
Suttanta and Abhidhamma methods, on Buddhist pheno- 
menalism, on Nibbana, nnd on Paticcasamuppida ав 
Conditioned Genesis. So instructive, in fact, that we were 
glad to give effect to the learned doctor's hope that his 
remarks might be published, in Pali and in English, by 
appending at least the Pali text of them to this volume. 
And а translation of the bulk at least of his notes will, we 
hope, appear in the Journal. His MS., written in Burmese 
characters, was transcribed by Miss C. Dibben. 

I did not retain a copy of the questions submitted to 
these kind teachers; and inquiries, better calculated to 
elicit light on the strange, and to us often useless and 
tiresome, nature of the text-queries, will doubtless suggest 
themselves to critical readers. It had long seemed to me 
that the work, in common with some other numbers of 
Abhidhamma-Pitaka books, was more suggestive of в 
manual of logical exercises—or, at most, of applied logic— 
than of a compilation having the one aim of building up 
bhikkhus in solid and purified doctrine. There seemed to 


Introductory Note ix 


be, for aims so lofty, too great a chasm between much of 
this baffling and apparently sterile catechizing and the 
fruitful, edifying trend of Suttanta discourse. That the 
Yamaka was and ів held, not ns such a course in method 
only, but to be intended to elucidate sound and purified 
doctrine is clear from all three seta of replies. 

Those replies may very possibly not clear away our 
doubts ан to the use and profit of the шалу Yamaka, or 
justify its existence in our judgment. I say thie with some 
diffidence, in view of Aletawya Sadaw's remark, that ' the 
intelligent, once versed in Yamaka method, can recite the 
text without any difficulty; but the stupid would make no 
hend or tail out of it, even if it were committed to memory pn 
But whatever may be thought of a ‘ London Pali-devi's' 
questions or of the replies, readers will share with ug in 
the vivid appreciation of the ready and unstinted and most 
kindly courtesy with which these distinguished representa- 
tives of the Burmese traditions of Buddhist culture—now 
so venerable and so continuous in its procession—have 
received und responded to our inquiries. 1 have been 
profoundly touched thereby, and rejoice to do all in my 
power to record and establish so auspicious an advance in 
intellectual intercourse between East and West. It may 
be a long day yet ere we derive much light from the 
Yamaka, but this gracious response to our questions, borne 
hither from the groves of Maramma, gilds the venerable 
catechism with a living luminance of human fraternity, 
even as, in great Moggallina’s judgment, the beauty of the 
moonlit grove of Gosinga Hall was enhanced when ‘ two 
talking Abhidhamma, asking each other questions and 
aneworing unfalteringly, their righteous discourse holds on 
its way." * 

The gist of the Notes supplied by Mr. Aung, and of those 
sent shortly after by Ingan Sadaw, is as follows: 


1 J.P.T.S., 1912, p. 118: ' Abhidbamma Literature in 
Burma,’ by S. Z. Aung. 
з Majjhima, i., 214. 


x Introductory Note 


Object of the Yamaka.—Standing where it does as sixth 
book in the Abhidhamma-Pitaka, after the first four books . 
of expository matter, and the fifth book which corrects 
perverted views on various points therein, the Yamaka 
aims at expurgating doubt by affirmations concerning 8 
number of matters wherein doubts may arise. Негеоп 
Anand’ Acariya’s Malafika is quoted.’ 

The subject-matter or argument of the Yamaka? The 
replies simply analyze briefly the contents of the ten books 
without attempting any more general summarization. 

The choice of the ten categories out of others, and of their 
order. The first half was perhaps scarcely a legitimate 
inquiry. Desand-rildsena ceva puggalajjharayarasena ca, 
Paticca-samuppadadike dhamme abhajetva . . . is the Thera's 
reply. The fundamental nature of the Málas naturally 
places them first (N.B., prior to Sankhara or Citta), The 
second, third, and fourth Yamakas follow the classical 
order; and that these should become profitable by being 
considered under a causal and dynamic aspect, the Sacca- 
Yamaka comes next . . . and so on. 

How can it profit students to learn affirmations concerning 
the psychology of beings in the | unconscious. sphere’ or the 
(€ Pure Abodes'? (See Yamaka i., рр. 19, 21,25, etc.) It 
is interesting, if disappointing, to note that the Thera, in 
a brief reply, does not understand the question as at all 
sceptically put, but answers, so to speak, by the book, 
quoting from the Paicappakaranatthalathà and the Mila- 
пка. Mr. Aung replies: ' I think that the inquiry into the 
psychology of gods and Brahms is intended to complete 
Buddhist philosophy. For example, there is no mind in 
the Asaünasatte plane, and the interval of 500 kalpas? is

simply bridged over by the Anantara-paccaya-satti of mind у 
as when a man, who loses his senso of personal identity for 
three years, rosumes it at the point where he left it.’ 

The reason for the great prolixity and redundancy in the 
Yamak meth. Ingan Sadaw makes here no reply. 
Mr. Aung reminds me that euch is the rule in Buddhist 
books, and that the rensons for it are well known to us. 

It will be seen, in the Appendix, that whereas the penult- 
imate question has failed either to reach Ledi Sadaw, or, 
anyway, to elicit a response, he also repudiates, for the 
Yamaka, any unreasonable amount of ‘vain repetitions.’ 
It is Abhidhamma, and not Suttanta. In the latter, 
instruction is adapted to individuale and special needs. 
In Abhidbamma exhaustive analysis, to be valid for anyone 
at all times, ia the rule (p. 232 f.).

A DISSERTATION ON THE YAMAKA, 
containing replies to inquiries as to the traditional view held 
in Burmese culture on the scope and method of the work, and 
including discussions on sume psychological and philosophical 

principles. 


By 
THE REVEREND DR. LEDI THERA 
OF MANDALAY.

! Note.—I wish to take upon myself the responsibility 
for any inaccuracy in the transcription and printing of 
this dissertation. The author has not had the proofs sub- 
mitted to him, and had made no request to that effect; 
indeed, I imagine he is not conversant with the Roman 
characters. I have indicated by footnotes some of the 
peculiarities of the Burmese traditional methods of Tali 
orthography ; these may be compared with the table given 
by Rhys Davids in the preface to the Swnangala Vilasini, 
vol. i., Р.Т.8. ed.—C. Ry. D.
